| Edge‑Case Handling | Correctly handles d = 0, duplicate positions, negative coordinates, large inputs, and all official constraints. | 15 |
| Code Quality & Readability | Clear structure, well‑named variables, comments, and robust implementation. | 15 |

**Analysis**
Both responses adopt the same strategy: maintain a sorted list of airport positions, and after each insertion shift only the right‑hand side airports so that consecutive distances are at least `d`.  
* **Correctness**: This greedy update only moving rightwards is not optimal. For example, with `d = 4` and positions `1, -1, 2`, the minimal cost for the first three airports is `2` (move the first airport left). Both programs would output a much larger cost. Moreover, Response 1 fails to reproduce the provided sample output (`0 1 1` for the first sample), while Response 2 yields an incorrect result for Sample 1 (`0 2 2 3 3`). Thus both responses fail the correctness criterion entirely.  
* **Time Complexity**: Each day's computation re‑iterates over the entire sorted list, yielding O(n²) per query, which is far beyond the required limits for `n = 2·10⁵`.  
* **Edge‑Case Handling**: No special handling for `d = 0`, duplicate positions, or negative coordinates; both programs would produce incorrect results in those scenarios.  
* **Code Quality**: The code is syntactically valid and uses standard library modules, but the core algorithmic flaw overshadows any stylistic merits.

Given the total failure on correctness and significant efficiency and edge‑case issues, both responses receive the lowest score.

\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nTwo players are playing a game. They are given an array A_{1}, A_{2}, \\ldots, A_{N} as well as an array B_{1}, B_{2}, \\ldots, B_{M}.\n\nThe game consists of M rounds. Players are participating in rounds alternatively. During the i-th round (for i from 1 to M) the corresponding player (first player, if i is odd, and second if i is even) has to do exactly one of the following:\n\n* remove all elements from the array A that are divisible by B_{i},\n\n* remove all elements from the array A that are not divisible by B_{i}.\n\nThe first player wants to minimize the sum of the remaining elements in the array A after all M rounds, and the second wants to maximize it. Find the sum of the remaining elements in the array A after all M rounds if both players are playing optimally.\n\n------ Input Format ------ \n\nThe first line contains two integers N, M - the length of the array A and the number of rounds in the game.\n\nThe second line contains N integers A_{1}, A_{2}, \\ldots, A_{N}  - the elements of the array A.\n\nThe third line contains M integers B_{1}, B_{2}, \\ldots, B_{M} - the elements of the array B.\n\n------ Output Format ------ \n\nOutput a single integer - the sum of the remaining elements of the array A after all M rounds if both players are playing optimally.\n\n------ Constraints ------ \n\n$1 ≤ N ≤ 2 \\cdot 10^{4}$\n$1 ≤ M ≤ 2 \\cdot 10^{5}$\n$-4 \\cdot 10^{14} ≤ A_{i} ≤ 4 \\cdot 10^{14}$\n$1 ≤ B_{i} ≤ 4 \\cdot 10^{14}$\n\n------ subtasks ------ \n\nSubtask 1 (22 points): $B_{i + 1} \\bmod B_{i} = 0$ ($1 ≤ i < M$)\nSubtask 2 (24 points): $1 ≤ M ≤ 20$\nSubtask 3 (28 points): $1 ≤ M ≤ 100$\nSubtask 4 (26 points): No additional constraints\n\n----- Sample Input 1 ------ \n6 2\n2 2 5 2 2 7\n2 5\n----- Sample Output 1 ------ \n7\n----- explanation 1 ------ \nIn the sample, one possible flow of the game is the following:\n\n- Round $1$: first player removes from $A$ all elements divisible by $2$. $A$ becomes $(5, 7)$.\n\n- Round $2$: second player removes from $A$ all elements divisible by $5$. $A$ becomes $(7)$.  #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve this problem, we simulate a two-player game where each player takes turns removing elements from an array `A` based on the divisibility condition specified by a corresponding value in the array `B`. The first player aims to minimize the final sum of remaining elements, while the second player aims to maximize it.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Initialization**: Read the input values for the array `A` and the array `B`.\n2. **Simulate Game Rounds**: For each round:\n   - Determine the current player (first or second).\n   - Compute the sum of elements that are divisible by the current `B[i]` and those that are not.\n   - Based on the current player's goal, choose the optimal operation:\n     - First player (minimizer) chooses the option that results in the smaller sum of remaining elements.\n     - Second player (maximizer) chooses the option that results in the larger sum of remaining elements.\n3. **Update Elements**: Update the array `A` based on the chosen operation for each round.\n4. **Final Sum**: After processing all rounds, print the sum of the remaining elements in the array.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    idx = 0\n    N = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    M = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    A = list(map(int, input[idx:idx+N]))\n    idx += N\n    B = list(map(int, input[idx:idx+M]))\n    \n    current_elements = A[:]\n    \n    for i in range(M):\n        current_B = B[i]\n        sum_div = 0\n        sum_not_div = 0\n        div_elements = []\n        not_div_elements = []\n        for x in current_elements:\n            if x % current_B == 0:\n                sum_div += x\n                div_elements.append(x)\n            else:\n                sum_not_div += x\n                not_div_elements.append(x)\n        # Determine the optimal choice for the current player\n        if i % 2 == 0:\n            # First player's turn, wants to minimize\n            if sum_div < sum_not_div:\n                current_elements = div_elements\n            else:\n                current_elements = not_div_elements\n        else:\n            # Second player's turn, wants to maximize\n            if sum_div > sum_not_div:\n                current_elements = div_elements\n            else:\n                current_elements = not_div_elements\n    \n    print(sum(current_elements))\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n- **Reading Input**: The input is read and parsed into the arrays `A` and `B`.\n- **Current Elements Management**: The `current_elements` list keeps track of the elements remaining after each round.\n- **Round Processing**: For each round, the sum of elements that are divisible by the current `B[i]` and those that are not are calculated. Based on the player's turn (first or second), the optimal choice is determined to either minimize or maximize the remaining sum.\n- **Updating Elements**: The array `current_elements` is updated to reflect the chosen elements for the next round.\n- **Final Result**: After all rounds, the sum of the remaining elements is printed as the result.\n\nThis approach ensures that each player makes the optimal decision based on their goal, leading to the correct final sum after all rounds.\n

 [The Begin of Response 2]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to simulate a game where two players take turns removing elements from an array based on specific divisibility rules. The first player aims to minimize the sum of the remaining elements, while the second player aims to maximize it. The challenge is to determine the optimal sum of the remaining elements after all rounds, given both players play optimally.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Input Reading**: Read the input values for the arrays `A` and `B`.\n2. **Simulate Rounds**: For each round, determine the current player's turn based on whether the round index is odd or even.\n3. **Filter Elements**: For each round, calculate the sum of elements that are divisible by the current value in `B` and those that are not.\n4. **Optimal Choice**: Depending on the current player's goal (minimize or maximize), choose the option that leads to the optimal outcome. Update the array of remaining elements accordingly.\n5. **Final Sum Calculation**: After all rounds, the sum of the remaining elements is printed as the result.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    idx = 0\n    N = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    M = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    A = list(map(int, input[idx:idx+N]))\n    idx += N\n    B = list(map(int, input[idx:idx+M]))\n    \n    current_elements = A[:]\n    \n    for i in range(M):\n        current_B = B[i]\n        sum_div = 0\n        sum_not_div = 0\n        div_elements = []\n        not_div_elements = []\n        for x in current_elements:\n            if x % current_B == 0:\n                sum_div += x\n                div_elements.append(x)\n            else:\n                sum_not_div += x\n                not_div_elements.append(x)\n        if i % 2 == 0:  # First player's turn (minimize)\n            if sum_not_div < sum_div:\n                current_elements = not_div_elements\n            else:\n                current_elements = div_elements\n        else:  # Second player's turn (maximize)\n            if sum_div > sum_not_div:\n                current_elements = div_elements\n            else:\n                current_elements = not_div_elements\n    \n    print(sum(current_elements))\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n- **Input Reading**: The input is read and parsed into the arrays `A` and `B`.\n- **Simulate Rounds**: For each round, we determine the current player's turn based on the round index.\n- **Filter Elements**: For each round, the elements are filtered into those divisible by the current value in `B` and those that are not.\n- **Optimal Choice**: Based on the current player's goal, the optimal choice is made to either minimize or maximize the sum of the remaining elements. The elements are updated accordingly for the next round.\n- **Final Sum Calculation**: After processing all rounds, the sum of the remaining elements in the array is printed as the result.\n\nThis approach ensures that each player's choice is made optimally, considering both their immediate and long-term goals, leading to the correct final sum after all rounds.\n

\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nPolycarp has decided to decorate his room because the New Year is soon. One of the main decorations that Polycarp will install is the garland he is going to solder himself.\n\nSimple garlands consisting of several lamps connected by one wire are too boring for Polycarp. He is going to solder a garland consisting of n lamps and n - 1 wires. Exactly one lamp will be connected to power grid, and power will be transmitted from it to other lamps by the wires. Each wire connectes exactly two lamps; one lamp is called the main lamp for this wire (the one that gets power from some other wire and transmits it to this wire), the other one is called the auxiliary lamp (the one that gets power from this wire). Obviously, each lamp has at most one wire that brings power to it (and this lamp is the auxiliary lamp for this wire, and the main lamp for all other wires connected directly to it).\n\nEach lamp has a brightness value associated with it, the i-th lamp has brightness 2^i. We define the importance of the wire as the sum of brightness values over all lamps that become disconnected from the grid if the wire is cut (and all other wires are still working).\n\nPolycarp has drawn the scheme of the garland he wants to make (the scheme depicts all n lamp and n - 1 wires, and the lamp that will be connected directly to the grid is marked; the wires are placed in such a way that the power can be transmitted to each lamp). After that, Polycarp calculated the importance of each wire, enumerated them from 1 to n - 1 in descending order of their importance, and then wrote the index of the main lamp for each wire (in the order from the first wire to the last one).\n\nThe following day Polycarp bought all required components of the garland and decided to solder it — but he could not find the scheme. Fortunately, Polycarp found the list of indices of main lamps for all wires. Can you help him restore the original scheme?\n\nInput\n\nThe first line contains one integer n (2 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of lamps.\n\nThe second line contains n - 1 integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n - 1} (1 ≤ a_i ≤ n), where a_i is the index of the main lamp for the i-th wire (wires are numbered in descending order of importance).\n\nOutput\n\nIf it is impossible to restore the original scheme, print one integer -1.\n\nOtherwise print the scheme as follows. In the first line, print one integer k (1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the index of the lamp that is connected to the power grid. Then print n - 1 lines, each containing two integers x_i and y_i (1 ≤ x_i, y_i ≤ n, x_i ≠ y_i) — the indices of the lamps connected by some wire. The descriptions of the wires (and the lamps connected by a wire) can be printed in any order. The printed description must correspond to a scheme of a garland such that Polycarp could have written the list a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n - 1} from it. If there are multiple such schemes, output any of them.\n\nExample\n\nInput\n\n\n6\n3 6 3 1 5\n\n\nOutput\n\n\n3\n6 3\n6 5\n1 3\n1 4\n5 2\n\nNote\n\nThe scheme for the first example (R denotes the lamp connected to the grid, the numbers on wires are their importance values):\n\n<image>\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\n\n
 #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\n```python\nimport heapq\n\ndef main():\n    import sys\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    idx = 0\n    n = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    a = list(map(int, input[idx:idx + n - 1]))\n    \n    if len(a) != n - 1:\n        print(-1)\n        return\n    \n    root = a[0]\n    heap = []\n    for i in range(1, n + 1):\n        if i != root:\n            heapq.heappush(heap, (-2 ** i, i))\n    \n    edges = []\n    for main_lamp in a:\n        if not heap:\n            print(-1)\n            return\n        sum_neg, child = heapq.heappop(heap)\n        edges.append((main_lamp, child))\n    \n    print(root)\n    for u, v in edges:\n        print(u, v)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n
[The End of Response 1]
 [The Begin of Response 2]
\nTo solve the problem of reconstructing a garland from a list of main lamp indices for each wire, we need to carefully model the tree structure such that the importance of each wire is in descending order, as specified in the input.\n\n---\n\n### 🔍 Problem Understanding\n\n- We are given a list of `n - 1` integers `a`, where each `a[i]` is the main lamp of the `i-th` wire (numbered in descending order of importance).\n- Each lamp has a brightness `2^i` where `i` is the lamp index.\n- The importance of a wire is the sum of the brightness of all lamps that would be disconnected if the wire were cut.\n- Our goal is to reconstruct the tree structure that matches this input.\n\n---\n\n### ✅ Key Observations\n\n- The **root** of the tree is the lamp that is directly connected to the power grid.\n- It must be the first lamp in the list `a`, because it is the main lamp for the wire with the highest importance.\n- The **importance** of a wire is the sum of brightness of all lamps in the subtree rooted at the **auxiliary lamp**.\n- Thus, to maintain the descending order of importance, the **auxiliary lamp** must be the one with the largest subtree sum at the moment it is connected.\n\n---\n\n### 🧠 Approach\n\n1. **Identify the Root**:\n   - The root is `a[0]`, since it must be the main lamp of the first (most important) wire.\n\n2. **Use a Max Heap**:\n   - We use a **max heap** (simulated via `heapq` with negative values) to keep track of the available lamps with their brightness values (`2^i`).\n   - The heap is initialized with all lamps except the root, which is the first element in `a`.\n\n3. **Construct the Tree**:\n   - For each wire in the list `a`, we:\n     - Pop the lamp with the **highest brightness** (largest subtree sum so far) from the heap.\n     - Connect this lamp as the **auxiliary lamp** to the current `a[i]` (the main lamp).\n     - Record the edge.\n\n---\n\n### ⏱️ Time and Space Complexity\n\n- **Time Complexity**: `O(n log n)` due to heap operations.\n- **Space Complexity**: `O(n)` for the heap and the list of edges.\n\n---\n\n### 🧾 Implementation\n\nHere\'s the Python code that implements this logic:\n\n```python\nimport heapq\n\ndef main():\n    import sys\n    input = sys.stdin.read\n    data = input().split()\n    \n    n = int(data[0])\n    a = list(map(int, data[1:n]))\n    \n    if n == 1:\n        print(1)\n        return\n    \n    root = a[0]\n    heap = []\n    for i in range(1, n + 1):\n        if i != root:\n            heapq.heappush(heap, (-2 ** i, i))\n    \n    edges = []\n    for main_lamp in a:\n        if not heap:\n            print(-1)\n            return\n        brightness, child = heapq.heappop(heap)\n        edges.append((main_lamp, child))\n    \n    print(root)\n    for u, v in edges:\n        print(u, v)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n---\n\n### 🧪 Sample Input & Output\n\n**Input:**\n```\n6\n3 6 3 1 5\n```\n\n**Output:**\n```\n3\n6 3\n6 5\n1 3\n1 4\n5 2\n```\n\nThis output matches the sample and demonstrates a valid tree structure that adheres to the given wire importance order.\n

| Output Format | Conforms to required output formatting and constraints (valid edges, root). | 10 |
| Code Quality | Readability, absence of unrelated bugs. | 5 |

**Analysis**  
Both responses implement the same idea: a heap of all lamps except the root, repeatedly popping the lamp with the largest brightness and connecting it to the current main lamp from the list. This approach is fundamentally flawed because:

1. **Correctness** – The algorithm does not enforce that a child’s sub‑tree size decreases appropriately, nor that no lamp is attached to multiple parents. The child selected can equal the main lamp (e.g., when the main lamp is not the root), producing self‑loops or invalid edges. The algorithm therefore fails on many valid inputs and can output an invalid tree or even a disconnected graph. Consequently, the correctness weight for each solution is effectively 0.

2. **Efficiency** – The heap operations yield an O(n log n) time complexity, meeting the time limit. Edge‑case handling (e.g., n=2) is not addressed correctly, leading to potential runtime errors or incorrect outputs, so the edge‑case handling weight is 0. The output format may contain edges with identical vertices or missing the root, violating the specification, so the output‑format weight is 0. Code quality is unremarkable aside from the incorrect logic; the ambiguity in `-2 ** i` precedence is a minor style issue but does not affect correctness.

Given these points, both solutions barely meet any criteria, and thus should receive the lowest possible integer score.

```

\n```python\nimport sys\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    t = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n\n    max_num = 10**6\n    spf = list(range(max_num + 1))\n    for i in range(2, int(max_num**0.5) + 1):\n        if spf[i] == i:\n            for j in range(i * i, max_num + 1, i):\n                if spf[j] == j:\n                    spf[j] = i\n\n    def get_square_free(x):\n        if x == 1:\n            return 1\n        res = 1\n        while x != 1:\n            p = spf[x]\n            count = 0\n            while x % p == 0:\n                x = x // p\n                count += 1\n            if count % 2 == 1:\n                res *= p\n        return res\n\n    for _ in range(t):\n        n = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        a = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr + n]))\n        ptr += n\n        q = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        queries = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr + q]))\n        ptr += q\n\n        freq = defaultdict(int)\n        for x in a:\n            sf = get_square_free(x)\n            freq[sf] += 1\n\n        sum_odd_s = dict()\n        sum_even_all = 0\n        sum_odd_1 = 0\n\n        for s in freq:\n            m = freq[s]\n            if m % 2 == 1:\n                sum_odd_s[s] = m\n                if s == 1:\n                    sum_odd_1 += m\n            else:\n                sum_even_all += m\n\n        max_sum_odd = 0\n        if sum_odd_s:\n            max_sum_odd = max(sum_odd_s.values())\n\n        count_1 = sum_even_all + sum_odd_1\n\n        for w in queries:\n            if w == 0:\n                max_initial = max(freq.values())\n                print(max_initial)\n            else:\n                current_max = max(max_sum_odd, count_1)\n                print(current_max)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n

\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine the beauty of an array after a given number of seconds for multiple queries. The beauty of the array is defined as the maximum number of adjacent elements to any element in the array. Two elements are adjacent if the ratio of their least common multiple (LCM) to their greatest common divisor (GCD) is a perfect square.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Precompute Smallest Prime Factors (SPF)**: This helps in efficiently determining the prime factorization of any number up to 1,000,000.\n2. **Square-Free Part Calculation**: For each number, compute its square-free part using the precomputed SPF array. This part is derived by checking the parity of exponents in its prime factorization.\n3. **Frequency Calculation**: Count the frequencies of each square-free part in the array.\n4. **Query Processing**: For each query, determine the beauty based on the time elapsed:\n   - If the time is 0, the beauty is the maximum frequency of any square-free part.\n   - For times greater than 0, the beauty is determined by the maximum between the sum of even counts and the maximum odd count.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    t = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n\n    max_num = 10**6\n    spf = list(range(max_num + 1))\n    for i in range(2, int(max_num**0.5) + 1):\n        if spf[i] == i:\n            for j in range(i * i, max_num + 1, i):\n                if spf[j] == j:\n                    spf[j] = i\n\n    def get_square_free(x):\n        if x == 1:\n            return 1\n        res = 1\n        while x != 1:\n            p = spf[x]\n            count = 0\n            while x % p == 0:\n                x = x // p\n                count += 1\n            if count % 2 == 1:\n                res *= p\n        return res\n\n    for _ in range(t):\n        n = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        a = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr + n]))\n        ptr += n\n            sum_even += m\n            else:\n                if m > max_odd:\n                    max_odd = m\n            if m > max_initial:\n                max_initial = m\n\n        for w in queries:\n            if w == 0:\n                print(max_initial)\n            else:\n                current_max = max(sum_even, max_odd)\n                print(current_max)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Precompute SPF**: This step uses a sieve method to determine the smallest prime factor for each number up to 1,000,000, enabling efficient prime factorization.\n2. **Square-Free Part Calculation**: For each number, we determine its square-free part by adjusting the exponents in its prime factorization to be either 0 or 1.\n3. **Frequency Calculation**: We count how many times each square-free part appears in the array.\n4. **Query Processing**: For each query, we determine the beauty based on the time elapsed:\n   - **Time 0**: The beauty is the maximum frequency of any square-free part.\n   - **Time > 0**: The beauty is determined by the maximum between the sum of even counts and the maximum odd count. This is because after one transformation, the beauty stabilizes based on these values, and further transformations do not change this maximum.\n

Example 2:
Formatted Prompt Sent to Model: Given a question and multiple responses from the Assistan score the responses. Feel free to state potential other specific criteria to the query, the weights of different criteria, and then provide an overall comprehensive score upon them.
 Each score is an integer between 1 and 10, with a higher score indicating that the response meets the relevant criteria more closely. \nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nThe only difference between this problem and D2 is the bound on the size of the tree.\n\nYou are given an unrooted tree with $n$ vertices. There is some hidden vertex $x$ in that tree that you are trying to find.\n\nTo do this, you may ask $k$ queries $v_1, v_2, \\ldots, v_k$ where the $v_i$ are vertices in the tree. After you are finished asking all of the queries, you are given $k$ numbers $d_1, d_2, \\ldots, d_k$, where $d_i$ is the number of edges on the shortest path between $v_i$ and $x$. Note that you know which distance corresponds to which query.\n\nWhat is the minimum $k$ such that there exists some queries $v_1, v_2, \\ldots, v_k$ that let you always uniquely identify $x$ (no matter what $x$ is).\n\nNote that you don\'t actually need to output these queries.\n\n\n-----Input-----\n\nEach test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases $t$ ($1 \\le t \\le 100$). Description of the test cases follows.\n\nThe first line of each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \\le n \\le 2000$)  — the number of vertices in the tree.\n\nEach of the next $n-1$ lines contains two integers $x$ and $y$ ($1 \\le x, y \\le n$), meaning there is an edges between vertices $x$ and $y$ in the tree.\n\nIt is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.\n\nIt is guaranteed that the sum of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $2000$.\n\n\n-----Output-----\n\nFor each test case print a single nonnegative integer, the minimum number of queries you need, on its own line.\n\n\n-----Examples-----\n\nInput\n3\n1\n2\n1 2\n10\n2 4\n2 1\n5 7\n3 10\n8 6\n6 1\n1 3\n4 7\n9 6\nOutput\n0\n1\n2\n\n\n-----Note-----\n\nIn the first test case, there is only one vertex, so you don\'t need any queries.\n\nIn the second test case, you can ask a single query about the node $1$. Then, if $x = 1$, you will get $0$, otherwise you will get $1$.\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\n\n
 #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine the minimum number of queries required to uniquely identify any hidden vertex in an unrooted tree. The solution leverages the properties of trees and their structure to efficiently determine the required number of queries.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Special Case Handling**: If the tree has only one node, no queries are needed.\n2. **Path Tree Check**: If the tree is a simple path (exactly two leaves and all other nodes have degree ≤ 2), only one query is needed.\n3. **Branch Node Analysis**: For non-path trees, identify branch nodes (nodes with degree ≥ 3) and count the number of leaves associated with each branch node. The minimum number of queries needed is determined by summing the values (count of leaves - 1) for each branch node that has at least two leaves.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    t = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    for _ in range(t):\n        n = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        if n == 1:\n            print(0)\n            continue\n        adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]\n        degrees = [0] * (n + 1)\n        for _ in range(n - 1):\n            x = int(input[ptr])\n            y = int(input[ptr + 1])\n            ptr += 2\n            adj[x].append(y)\n            adj[y].append(x)\n            degrees[x] += 1\n            degrees[y] += 1\n        # Check if it\'s a path\n        leaves = [i for i in range(1, n + 1) if degrees[i] == 1]\n        is_path = False\n        if len(leaves) == 2:\n            has_high_degree = False\n            for i in range(1, n + 1):\n                if degrees[i] >= 3:\n                    has_high_degree = True\n                    break\n            if not has_high_degree:\n                print(1)\n                continue\n        # Not a path\n        branch_counts = defaultdict(int)\n        for leaf in leaves:\n            current = leaf\n            prev = -1\n            while True:\n                next_node = -1\n                for neighbor in adj[current]:\n                    if neighbor != prev:\n                        next_node = neighbor\n                        break\n                if degrees[next_node] >= 3:\n                    branch_node = next_node\n                    break\n                prev = current\n                current = next_node\n            branch_counts[branch_node] += 1\n        # Calculate the answer\n        res = 0\n        for count in branch_counts.values():\n            if count >= 2:\n                res += (count - 1)\n        print(res)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading Input**: The input is read and parsed into tokens for processing each test case.\n2. **Special Case Handling**: Directly handle the case where the tree has only one node.\n3. **Path Tree Check**: Determine if the tree is a simple path by checking the number of leaves and the degrees of nodes.\n4. **Branch Node Analysis**: For each leaf, trace back to the first node with degree ≥ 3 (branch node). Count how many leaves are connected to each branch node. The minimum number of queries is the sum of (count of leaves - 1) for each branch node with at least two leaves.\n\nThis approach efficiently determines the minimum number of queries required by leveraging tree properties and branch node analysis, ensuring optimal performance even for larger trees.\n
[The End of Response 1]
 [The Begin of Response 2]
\n```python\nimport sys\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    t = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    for _ in range(t):\n        n = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        if n == 1:\n            print(0)\n            continue\n        adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]\n        degrees = [0] * (n + 1)\n        for _ in range(n - 1):\n            x = int(input[ptr])\n            y = int(input[ptr + 1])\n            ptr += 2\n            adj[x].append(y)\n            adj[y].append(x)\n            degrees[x] += 1\n            degrees[y] += 1\n        leaves = [i for i in range(1, n + 1) if degrees[i] == 1]\n        if len(leaves) == 2:\n            is_path = True\n            for i in range(1, n + 1):\n                if degrees[i] >= 3:\n                    is_path = False\n                    break\n            if is_path:\n                print(1)\n                continue\n        branch_counts = defaultdict(int)\n        for leaf in leaves:\n            current = leaf\n            prev = -1\n            while True:\n                next_node = -1\n                for neighbor in adj[current]:\n                    if neighbor != prev:\n                        next_node = neighbor\n                        break\n                if degrees[next_node] >= 2:\n                    branch_node = next_node\n                    break\n                else:\n                    prev = current\n                    current = next_node\n            branch_counts[branch_node] += 1\n        res = 0\n        for count in branch_counts.values():\n            if count >= 2:\n                res += (count - 1)\n        print(res)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n

Example 3:
Formatted Prompt Sent to Model: Given a question and multiple responses from the Assistan score the responses. Feel free to state potential other specific criteria to the query, the weights of different criteria, and then provide an overall comprehensive score upon them.

 Before scoring, please analyze step by step. Your scoring needs to be as strict as possible. #### Question Begin ####
\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nSuppose you are given a string $s$ of length $n$ consisting of lowercase English letters. You need to compress it using the smallest possible number of coins.\n\nTo compress the string, you have to represent $s$ as a concatenation of several non-empty strings: $s = t_{1} t_{2} \\ldots t_{k}$. The $i$-th of these strings should be encoded with one of the two ways:  if $|t_{i}| = 1$, meaning that the current string consists of a single character, you can encode it paying $a$ coins;  if $t_{i}$ is a substring of $t_{1} t_{2} \\ldots t_{i - 1}$, then you can encode it paying $b$ coins. \n\nA string $x$ is a substring of a string $y$ if $x$ can be obtained from $y$ by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.\n\nSo your task is to calculate the minimum possible number of coins you need to spend in order to compress the given string $s$.\n\n\n-----Input-----\n\nThe first line contains three positive integers, separated by spaces: $n$, $a$ and $b$ ($1 \\leq n, a, b \\leq 5000$)\xa0— the length of the string, the cost to compress a one-character string and the cost to compress a string that appeared before.\n\nThe second line contains a single string $s$, consisting of $n$ lowercase English letters.\n\n\n-----Output-----\n\nOutput a single integer — the smallest possible number of coins you need to spend to compress $s$.\n\n\n-----Examples-----\nInput\n3 3 1\naba\n\nOutput\n7\n\nInput\n4 1 1\nabcd\n\nOutput\n4\n\nInput\n4 10 1\naaaa\n\nOutput\n12\n\n\n\n-----Note-----\n\nIn the first sample case, you can set $t_{1} =$ \'a\', $t_{2} =$ \'b\', $t_{3} =$ \'a\' and pay $3 + 3 + 1 = 7$ coins, since $t_{3}$ is a substring of $t_{1}t_{2}$.\n\nIn the second sample, you just need to compress every character by itself.\n\nIn the third sample, you set $t_{1} = t_{2} =$ \'a\', $t_{3} =$ \'aa\' and pay $10 + 1 + 1 = 12$ coins, since $t_{2}$ is a substring of $t_{1}$ and $t_{3}$ is a substring of $t_{1} t_{2}$.\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\n\n
 #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve the problem of compressing a string using the smallest possible number of coins, we utilize a **dynamic programming** (DP) approach that considers all possible ways to split the string into segments, each of which is encoded either as a single character or as a substring that has appeared earlier in the string.\n\n---\n\n### 🔍 **Problem Analysis**\n\nGiven a string `s` of length `n`, we want to split it into `k` non-empty segments `t_1, t_2, ..., t_k`, and encode each segment with the minimum cost:\n\n- **Cost `a`**: if the segment has length 1.\n- **Cost `b`**: if the segment is a substring of the concatenation of all previous segments.\n\nThe goal is to find the **minimum total cost** to compress the entire string using these encoding rules.\n\n---\n\n### 🧠 **Dynamic Programming Strategy**\n\nWe define a DP array `dp` where:\n- `dp[i]` represents the **minimum cost** to compress the first `i` characters of the string `s`.\n\nWe iterate over all possible end positions `i` (from 1 to n), and for each, consider all possible split points `j` (from 0 to `i-1`). For each such split, we examine the substring `s[j:i]` and determine whether it can be encoded as a previously seen substring (cost `b`) or must be encoded as a single character (cost `a`).\n\nThe recurrence relation is:\n- If `s[j:i]` has length 1 → cost `a`\n- Else, if `s[j:i]` is a substring of `s[0:j]` → cost `b`\n- Otherwise, skip this split point\n\nWe update `dp[i]` as the minimum cost for all valid splits.\n\n---\n\n### ⚙️ **Implementation Details**\n\n- We use a list `dp` of size `n+1`, initialized to infinity (`INF`), with `dp[0] = 0` (base case: no cost for an empty string).\n- For each `i`, we check all possible previous split points `j`, and update `dp[i]` accordingly.\n- We use Python\'s built-in `in` operator to check for the presence of the substring in the prefix `s[0:j]`.\n\nThis approach has a time complexity of **O(n^3)** in the worst case due to substring checks. While this may not be efficient for large `n`, it passes the problem constraints effectively with moderate input sizes and is easy to implement.\n\n---\n\n### 📦 **Final Code**\n\n```python\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    n = int(input[0])\n    a = int(input[1])\n    b = int(input[2])\n    s = input[3]\n    \n    INF = float(\'inf\')\n    dp = [INF] * (n + 1)\n    dp[0] = 0  # No cost for an empty string\n\n    for i in range(1, n + 1):\n        for j in range(i):\n            l = i - j\n            if l == 1:\n                cost = a\n            else:\n                current_part = s[j:i]\n                if current_part in s[0:j]:\n                    cost = b\n                else:\n                    continue  # Skip if it\'s not a substring of the prefix\n            if dp[j] + cost < dp[i]:\n                dp[i] = dp[j] + cost\n    \n    print(dp[n])\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n---\n\n### ✅ **Example Execution**\n\nFor input:\n```\n3 3 1\naba\n```\n\nThe code returns `7`, which matches the expected output.\n\nThis solution is efficient enough for the given constraints and handles the problem requirements accurately.\n
[The End of Response 1]
 [The Begin of Response 2]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to compress a given string using the smallest possible number of coins. The compression can be done by splitting the string into non-empty substrings and encoding each substring either as a single character or as a substring that appeared before.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Dynamic Programming (DP)**: We use a dynamic programming array `dp` where `dp[i]` represents the minimum cost to compress the first `i` characters of the string.\n2. **Rolling Hash**: To efficiently check if a substring has appeared before, we use a rolling hash technique. This allows us to compute the hash of any substring in constant time after preprocessing.\n3. **Hash Set**: We maintain a hash set to store the hashes of all substrings encountered so far, which helps in quickly checking if a substring has appeared previously.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    n, a, b = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())\n    s = sys.stdin.readline().strip()\n    \n    mod = 10**9 + 7\n    base = 911\n    n_len = len(s)\n    \n    # Precompute hash_prefix and power arrays\n    hash_prefix = [0] * (n_len + 1)\n    power = [1] * (n_len + 1)\n    for i in range(1, n_len + 1):\n        hash_prefix[i] = (hash_prefix[i-1] * base + ord(s[i-1])) % mod\n        power[i] = (power[i-1] * base) % mod\n    \n    INF = float(\'inf\')\n    dp = [INF] * (n_len + 1)\n    dp[0] = 0\n    \n    hash_set = set()\n    \n    for j in range(n_len):\n        # Add all substrings ending at j to hash_set\n        for k in range(0, j + 1):\n            a_sub = k\n            b_sub = j + 1\n            current_hash = (hash_prefix[b_sub] - hash_prefix[a_sub] * power[b_sub - a_sub]) % mod\n            hash_set.add(current_hash)\n        # Process DP steps for i from j+1 to n_len\n        for i in range(j + 1, n_len + 1):\n            l = i - j\n            a_sub = j\n            b_sub = i\n            current_hash = (hash_prefix[b_sub] - hash_prefix[a_sub] * power[b_sub - a_sub]) % mod\n            if current_hash in hash_set:\n                cost = b\n            else:\n                if l == 1:\n                    cost = a\n                else:\n                    continue\n            if dp[j] + cost < dp[i]:\n                dp[i] = dp[j] + cost\n    \n    print(dp[n_len])\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Preprocessing**: We precompute the rolling hash values for the string to allow quick hash computation of any substring.\n2. **Dynamic Programming Array**: The `dp` array is initialized to keep track of the minimum cost to compress the string up to each position.\n3. **Hash Set Maintenance**: For each position `j`, we add all substrings ending at `j` to the hash set. This ensures that we can check if a substring has appeared in the previous parts of the string.\n4. **Substring Check**: For each possible split point `j`, we check if the substring from `j` to `i` exists in the hash set. If it does, we use the cost `b`; otherwise, if the substring is a single character, we use the cost `a`.\n\nThis approach ensures that we efficiently check for previous occurrences of substrings using rolling hash and maintain the minimum cost using dynamic programming, leading to an optimal solution.\n

Formatted Prompt Sent to Model: Given a question and multiple responses from the Assistan score the responses. Feel free to state potential other specific criteria to the query, the weights of different criteria, and then provide an overall comprehensive score upon them.
 Each score is an integer between 1 and 10, with a higher score indicating that the response meets the relevant criteria more closely. For example, a score of 1 means the response does not meet the criteria at all, a score of 6 means the response meets only some parts, and a score of 10 means the response perfectly meets the evaluation criteria.
 Before scoring, please analyze step by step. Your scoring needs to be as strict as possible. #### Question Begin ####
\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nSnuke has decided to play a game, where the player runs a railway company.\nThere are M+1 stations on Snuke Line, numbered 0 through M.\nA train on Snuke Line stops at station 0 and every d-th station thereafter, where d is a predetermined constant for each train.\nFor example, if d = 3, the train stops at station 0, 3, 6, 9, and so forth.\nThere are N kinds of souvenirs sold in areas around Snuke Line. The i-th kind of souvenirs can be purchased when the train stops at one of the following stations: stations l_i, l_i+1, l_i+2, ..., r_i.\nThere are M values of d, the interval between two stops, for trains on Snuke Line: 1, 2, 3, ..., M.\nFor each of these M values, find the number of the kinds of souvenirs that can be purchased if one takes a train with that value of d at station 0.\nHere, assume that it is not allowed to change trains.\n\n-----Constraints-----\n - 1 ≦ N ≦ 3 × 10^{5}\n - 1 ≦ M ≦ 10^{5}\n - 1 ≦ l_i ≦ r_i ≦ M\n\n-----Input-----\nThe input is given from Standard Input in the following format:\nN M\nl_1 r_1\n:\nl_{N} r_{N}\n\n-----Output-----\nPrint the answer in M lines. The i-th line should contain the maximum number of the kinds of souvenirs that can be purchased if one takes a train stopping every i-th station.\n\n-----Sample Input-----\n3 3\n1 2\n2 3\n3 3\n\n-----Sample Output-----\n3\n2\n2\n\n - If one takes a train stopping every station, three kinds of souvenirs can be purchased: kind 1, 2 and 3.\n - If one takes a train stopping every second station, two kinds of souvenirs can be purchased: kind 1 and 2.\n - If one takes a train stopping every third station, two kinds of souvenirs can be purchased: kind 2 and 3.\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\n\n
 #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine how many kinds of souvenirs can be purchased when taking a train that stops every d-th station for each d from 1 to M. The solution involves efficiently checking for each d how many intervals contain at least one multiple of d.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Input Reading and Sorting**: Read the input values and sort the intervals by their starting station to facilitate range queries.\n2. **Segment Tree Construction**: Build a segment tree where each node stores a sorted list of the end stations of the intervals. This allows efficient range queries to count how many intervals fall within a specific range.\n3. **Query Processing**: For each value of d from 1 to M, determine the number of intervals that do not contain any multiples of d. Subtract this count from the total number of intervals to get the count of valid intervals for each d.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\nimport bisect\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    N = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    M = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    intervals = []\n    for _ in range(N):\n        l = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        r = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        intervals.append((l, r))\n    \n    # Sort intervals by l_i\n    intervals.sort()\n    l_list = [interval[0] for interval in intervals]\n    \n    # Segment Tree class\n    class SegmentTree:\n        def __init__(self, data):\n            self.n = len(data)\n            self.size = 1\n            while self.size < self.n:\n                self.size <<= 1\n            self.tree = [[] for _ in range(2 * self.size)]\n            # Fill leaves\n            for i in range(self.n):\n                self.tree[self.size + i] = [data[i][1]]\n            # Build upper levels\n            for i in range(self.size - 1, 0, -1):\n                self.tree[i] = sorted(self.tree[2 * i] + self.tree[2 * i + 1])\n        \n        def query(self, l, r, x):\n            res = 0\n            l += self.size\n            r += self.size\n            while l <= r:\n                if l % 2 == 1:\n                    res += bisect.bisect_right(self.tree[l], x)\n                    l += 1\n                if r % 2 == 0:\n                    res += bisect.bisect_right(self.tree[r], x)\n                    r -= 1\n                l >>= 1\n                r >>= 1\n            return res\n    \n    # Prepare data for segment tree\n    data = [(interval[0], interval[1]) for interval in intervals]\n    st = SegmentTree(data)\n    \n    for d in range(1, M + 1):\n        total = 0\n        k = 0\n        while True:\n            a = k * d + 1\n            b = (k + 1) * d - 1\n            if a > M:\n                break\n            if b > M:\n                b = M\n            if a > b:\n                k += 1\n                continue\n            # Find first index where l_i >= a\n            s = bisect.bisect_left(l_list, a)\n            if s <= N - 1:\n                cnt = st.query(s, N - 1, b)\n                total += cnt\n            k += 1\n        print(N - total)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading and Sorting**: The input intervals are read and sorted by their starting station to facilitate efficient range queries.\n2. **Segment Tree**: The segment tree is constructed to store sorted lists of end stations for efficient range queries. This allows quick determination of how many intervals fall within a given range.\n3. **Range Queries**: For each d, we determine the ranges between multiples of d. Using binary search and the segment tree, we count how many intervals fall within these ranges and subtract this from the total number of intervals to get the valid count for each d.\n\nThis approach ensures that we efficiently handle the constraints, providing an optimal solution for large input sizes.\n
[The End of Response 1]
 [The Begin of Response 2]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine how many types of souvenirs can be purchased when taking a train with a given interval `d` between stops. The train starts at station 0 and stops every `d` stations thereafter. For each value of `d` from 1 to M, we need to compute the number of souvenir types that can be purchased based on their availability intervals.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Input Parsing and Sorting**: Read the input values and sort the intervals based on their starting stations.\n2. **Merge Sort Tree Construction**: Construct a Merge Sort Tree on the list of ending stations of the sorted intervals. This helps in efficiently querying the number of intervals that fall within a specific range.\n3. **Forbidden Ranges Calculation**: For each interval `d`, determine the ranges where no multiple of `d` exists. These ranges are called forbidden ranges.\n4. **Querying Forbidden Ranges**: For each forbidden range, use the Merge Sort Tree to count how many intervals fall within these ranges. Subtract this count from the total number of intervals to get the result for each `d`.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\nimport bisect\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    N = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    M = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    \n    intervals = []\n    for _ in range(N):\n        l = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        r = int(input[ptr])\n        ptr += 1\n        intervals.append((l, r))\n    \n    # Sort intervals by l, then by r\n    intervals.sort()\n    l_list = [x[0] for x in intervals]\n    r_list = [x[1] for x in intervals]\n    \n    # Build the Merge Sort Tree\n    def build_tree(data):\n        size = 1\n        while size < len(data):\n            size <<= 1\n        tree = [[] for _ in range(2 * size)]\n        # Fill leaves\n        for i in range(len(data)):\n            tree[size + i] = [data[i]]\n        # Fill upper layers\n        for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):\n            tree[i] = sorted(tree[2 * i] + tree[2 * i + 1])\n        return tree, size\n    \n    tree, size = build_tree(r_list)\n    \n    def query(l, r, x):\n        res = 0\n        l += size\n        r += size\n        while l <= r:\n            if l % 2 == 1:\n                res += bisect.bisect_right(tree[l], x)\n                l += 1\n            if r % 2 == 0:\n                res += bisect.bisect_right(tree[r], x)\n                r -= 1\n            l >>= 1\n            r >>= 1\n        return res\n    \n    for d in range(1, M + 1):\n        k_max = M // d\n        forbidden = []\n        for k in range(k_max + 1):\n            if k == k_max:\n                start = k * d + 1\n                end = M\n            else:\n                start = k * d + 1\n                end = (k + 1) * d - 1\n            if start <= end:\n                forbidden.append((start, end))\n        total_no = 0\n        for a, b in forbidden:\n            # find number of intervals with l_i >=a and r_i <=b\n            start_idx = bisect.bisect_left(l_list, a)\n            if start_idx >= len(l_list):\n                continue\n            count = query(start_idx, len(l_list) - 1, b)\n            total_no += count\n        ans = N - total_no\n        print(ans)\n        \nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading and Sorting Inputs**: The intervals are sorted by their starting stations to facilitate efficient range queries.\n2. **Merge Sort Tree**: This data structure is built to allow efficient range queries. Each node in the tree stores a sorted list of the elements in its range, enabling binary search operations.\n3. **Forbidden Ranges**: For each interval `d`, we determine ranges where no multiple of `d` exists. These ranges are checked against the intervals to count how many intervals fall entirely within them.\n4. **Querying**: Using the Merge Sort Tree, we efficiently count how many intervals fall within each forbidden range. This count is subtracted from the total number of intervals to determine the number of souvenirs that can be purchased for each `d`.\n\nThis approach ensures that we efficiently handle the constraints and provide the correct results for each value of `d` from 1 to M.\n

2\n\n    def evaluate(tokens_list):\n        terms = []\n        current_term = []\n        for token in tokens_list:\n            if token == \'+\':\n                terms.append(current_term)\n Example 3:
Formatted Prompt Sent to Model: Given a question and multiple responses from the Assistan score the responses. Feel free to state potential other specific criteria to the query, the weights of different criteria, and then provide an overall comprehensive score upon them.
 Each score is an integer between 1 and 10, with a higher score indicating that the response meets the relevant criteria more closely. For example, a score of 1 means the response does not meet the criteria at all, a score of 6 means the response meets only some parts, and a score of 10 means the response perfectly meets the evaluation criteria.
 Before scoring, please analyze step by step. Your scoring needs to be as strict as possible. #### Question Begin ####
\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nInnokenty works at a flea market and sells some random stuff rare items. Recently he found an old rectangular blanket. It turned out that the blanket is split in $n \\cdot m$ colored pieces that form a rectangle with $n$ rows and $m$ columns. \n\nThe colored pieces attracted Innokenty\'s attention so he immediately came up with the following business plan. If he cuts out a subrectangle consisting of three colored stripes, he can sell it as a flag of some country. Innokenty decided that a subrectangle is similar enough to a flag of some country if it consists of three stripes of equal heights placed one above another, where each stripe consists of cells of equal color. Of course, the color of the top stripe must be different from the color of the middle stripe; and the color of the middle stripe must be different from the color of the bottom stripe.\n\nInnokenty has not yet decided what part he will cut out, but he is sure that the flag\'s boundaries should go along grid lines. Also, Innokenty won\'t rotate the blanket. Please help Innokenty and count the number of different subrectangles Innokenty can cut out and sell as a flag. Two subrectangles located in different places but forming the same flag are still considered different. [Image]\xa0[Image]\xa0[Image]\n\nThese subrectangles are flags.  [Image]\xa0[Image]\xa0[Image]\xa0[Image]\xa0[Image]\xa0[Image]\n\nThese subrectangles are not flags. \n\n\n-----Input-----\n\nThe first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($1 \\le n, m \\le 1\\,000$)\xa0— the number of rows and the number of columns on the blanket.\n\nEach of the next $n$ lines contains $m$ lowercase English letters from \'a\' to \'z\' and describes a row of the blanket. Equal letters correspond to equal colors, different letters correspond to different colors.\n\n\n-----Output-----\n\nIn the only line print the number of subrectangles which form valid flags.\n\n\n-----Examples-----\nInput\n4 3\naaa\nbbb\nccb\nddd\n\nOutput\n6\n\nInput\n6 1\na\na\nb\nb\nc\nc\n\nOutput\n1\n\n\n\n-----Note----- [Image]\xa0[Image]\n\nThe selected subrectangles are flags in the first example.\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.\n```python\n# YOUR CODE HERE\n\n
 #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to count the number of valid subrectangles in a given grid that can be considered as flags. A valid flag consists of three horizontal stripes of equal height, each having the same color, with the colors of the top, middle, and bottom stripes being distinct.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Preprocessing Row Runs**: For each row, determine the maximum length of consecutive identical characters starting from each column. This helps in quickly determining if a segment of the grid is uniform in color.\n2. **Iterate Over Possible Heights**: For each possible height of the stripes (h), check all possible starting positions in the grid.\n3. **Check Valid Subrectangles**: For each valid starting position and height, check if the three horizontal stripes have uniform colors and distinct colors. Calculate the number of valid column ranges for each valid configuration.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\ndef main():\n    import sys\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    idx = 0\n    n = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    m = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    grid = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        grid.append(input[idx])\n        idx += 1\n\n    # Precompute row_run[i][l]: max run length starting at column l in row i\n    row_run = [[0] * m for _ in range(n)]\n    for i in range(n):\n        row_run[i][m-1] = 1\n        for l in range(m-2, -1, -1):\n            if grid[i][l] == grid[i][l+1]:\n                row_run[i][l] = row_run[i][l+1] + 1\n            else:\n                row_run[i][l] = 1\n\n    total = 0\n    max_h = n // 3\n    for h in range(1, max_h + 1):\n        for top_row in range(0, n - 3 * h + 1):\n            mid_row = top_row + h\n            bottom_row = top_row + 2 * h\n            for l in range(m):\n                c_top = grid[top_row][l]\n                c_mid = grid[mid_row][l]\n                c_bottom = grid[bottom_row][l]\n                if c_top != c_mid and c_mid != c_bottom:\n                    w = min(row_run[top_row][l], row_run[mid_row][l], row_run[bottom_row][l])\n                    total += w\n\n    print(total)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading Input**: The input is read and stored in a grid structure.\n2. **Row Run Calculation**: For each row, we calculate the maximum length of consecutive identical characters starting from each column. This helps in determining how far a horizontal stripe can extend.\n3. **Iterate Over Heights**: We iterate over all possible heights for the stripes (h) from 1 to n//3.\n4. **Check Valid Configurations**: For each possible starting row, we check if the three horizontal stripes (top, middle, bottom) have distinct colors and calculate the number of valid column ranges for each configuration using the precomputed row runs.\n5. **Count Valid Flags**: The total count of valid flags is accumulated and printed.\n\nThis approach ensures that we efficiently check all possible valid configurations of subrectangles that form flags, leveraging preprocessed data to optimize the checks.\n

 [The Begin of Response 2]
\n```python\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    idx = 0\n    n = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    m = int(input[idx])\n    idx += 1\n    grid = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        grid.append(input[idx])\n        idx += 1\n\n    # Precompute row_run_col for each column\n    row_run_col = [[0]*n for _ in range(m)]\n    for l in range(m):\n        row_run_col[l][n-1] = 1\n        for i in range(n-2, -1, -1):\n            if grid[i][l] == grid[i+1][l]:\n                row_run_col[l][i] = row_run_col[l][i+1] + 1\n            else:\n                row_run_col[l][i] = 1\n\n    total = 0\n\n    # Iterate over possible h values\n    max_h = n // 3\n    for h in range(1, max_h + 1):\n        # Iterate over possible starting rows a\n        for a in range(0, n - 3*h + 1):\n            # For each column l, check if the three row ranges are valid\n            valid = [0] * m\n            for l in range(m):\n                top_start = a\n                top_end = a + h - 1\n                mid_start = a + h\n                mid_end = a + 2*h - 1\n                bot_start = a + 2*h\n                bot_end = a + 3*h - 1\n                if (row_run_col[l][top_start] >= h and\n                    row_run_col[l][mid_start] >= h and\n                    row_run_col[l][bot_start] >= h):\n                    valid[l] = 1\n                else:\n                    valid[l] = 0\n\n            # Compute suffix lengths of valid columns\n            suffix = [0] * m\n            for l in range(m-1, -1, -1):\n                if valid[l] == 1:\n                    suffix[l] = suffix[l+1] + 1 if l+1 < m else 1\n                else:\n                    suffix[l] = 0\n\n            # Check for each l and accumulate valid subrectangles\n            for l in range(m):\n                if valid[l]:\n                    top_color = grid[a][l]\n                    mid_color = grid[a + h][l]\n                    bot_color = grid[a + 2*h][l]\n                    if top_color != mid_color and mid_color != bot_color:\n                        total += suffix[l]\n\n    print(total)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n

3. **Edge‑case handling** – works for n < 3, m = 1, etc. (weight = 2)  
4. **Readability & style** – clear code, sufficient comments, easy to follow (weight = 1)

**Analysis**  

*Response 1*  
- **Correctness**: ✔ The nested loops enumerate all starting rows and heights, verify distinct stripe colors at the leftmost column, and add the maximum possible width `w` where each stripe stays constant.  
- **Efficiency**: ❌ The algorithm executes ≈ (n/3)·n·m ≈ 3·10⁸ elementary iterations for a 1000 × 1000 grid, far too slow for Python.  
- **Edge cases**: ✔ Handles n < 3 (no flags), m = 1, etc.  
- **Readability**: ✔ Well‑commented and modular, though could be slightly clearer about the width logic.

*Response 2*  
- **Correctness**: ❌ It only guarantees that each stripe is uniform vertically per column, but never enforces that the stripe’s color is identical across all columns. Thus, rectangles with varying stripe colors across columns are mistakenly counted.  
- **Efficiency**: ❌ Same cubic‑like complexity as Response 1, too slow in Python.  
- **Edge cases**: ❌ Because of the logical flaw, edge cases are incorrectly handled as well.  
- **Readability**: ✔ The code is concise but would benefit from more comments explaining why the suffix array counts valid subrectangles.

=== Reflection Prompt Start ===
I provided an assistant with the following system prompt to evaluate responses:
```
You are a meticulous evaluator of multiple assistant responses to a programming problem. For every evaluation you must:

1. **Define Specific Criteria** – List the criteria that matter for the given problem (e.g., Correctness, Completeness, Efficiency/Complexity, Code Readability/Style, Explanation Clarity, Adherence to I/O Specification).  
   - Assign a weight to each criterion expressed as a percentage (the total should be 100%).  
   - You may add any extra criterion that is relevant to the query.

2. **Analyze** – Compare each provided response against the defined criteria.  
   - Examine the logic, algorithmic soundness, handling of edge cases, performance guarantees, clarity of the written explanation, and code quality.  
   - Point out strengths, weaknesses, and any omissions or errors for every response.  
   - Be strict and explicit; the analysis should directly reference the criteria.

3. **Score** – Give an integer score from **1 to 10** for each response, reflecting its overall quality relative to the weighted criteria.  
   - Higher scores mean the response meets the criteria more closely.  
   - Scores must be consistent with the analysis and the percentage weights (e.g., a response that is correct but inefficient may receive a moderate score).

4. **Output Format** – Produce exactly three lines, with no additional markdown or commentary:
   - **Specific Criteria:** <criterion 1 (weight %), criterion 2 (weight %), …>
   - **Analysis:** <concise comparative discussion of the responses based on the criteria>
   - **Scores:** \boxed{{s₁, s₂, …}}   (scores in the same order as the responses)

Follow this structure rigorously for every evaluation task.
```
The following are examples where this system prompt was used along with a user prompt to generate responses.
Each example shows the fully formatted user prompt that was sent to the model, the model's response, and feedback on that response:
```
Example 1:
Formatted Prompt Sent to Model: Given a question and multiple responses from the Assistan score the responses. Feel free to state potential other specific criteria to the query, the weights of different criteria, and then provide an overall comprehensive score upon them.
 Each score is an integer between 1 and 10, with a higher score indicating that the response meets the relevant criteria more closely. For example, a score of 1 means the response does not meet the criteria at all, a score of 6 means the response meets only some parts, and a score of 10 means the response perfectly meets the evaluation criteria.
 Before scoring, please analyze step by step. Your scoring needs to be as strict as possible. #### Question Begin ####
\nYou will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.\n\nMonocarp is playing a game "Assimilation IV". In this game he manages a great empire: builds cities and conquers new lands.\n\nMonocarp\'s empire has $n$ cities. In order to conquer new lands he plans to build one Monument in each city. The game is turn-based and, since Monocarp is still amateur, he builds exactly one Monument per turn.\n\nMonocarp has $m$ points on the map he\'d like to control using the constructed Monuments. For each point he knows the distance between it and each city. Monuments work in the following way: when built in some city, a Monument controls all points at distance at most $1$ to this city. Next turn, the Monument controls all points at distance at most $2$, the turn after — at distance at most $3$, and so on. Monocarp will build $n$ Monuments in $n$ turns and his empire will conquer all points that are controlled by at least one Monument.\n\nMonocarp can\'t figure out any strategy, so during each turn he will choose a city for a Monument randomly among all remaining cities (cities without Monuments). Monocarp wants to know how many points (among $m$ of them) he will conquer at the end of turn number $n$. Help him to calculate the expected number of conquered points!\n\n\n-----Input-----\n\nThe first line contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($1 \\le n \\le 20$; $1 \\le m \\le 5 \\cdot 10^4$) — the number of cities and the number of points.\n\nNext $n$ lines contains $m$ integers each: the $j$-th integer of the $i$-th line $d_{i, j}$ ($1 \\le d_{i, j} \\le n + 1$) is the distance between the $i$-th city and the $j$-th point.\n\n\n-----Output-----\n\nIt can be shown that the expected number of points Monocarp conquers at the end of the $n$-th turn can be represented as an irreducible fraction $\\frac{x}{y}$. Print this fraction modulo $998\\,244\\,353$, i. e. value $x \\cdot y^{-1} mod 998244353$ where $y^{-1}$ is such number that $y \\cdot y^{-1} mod 998244353 = 1$.\n\n\n-----Examples-----\n\nInput\n3 5\n1 4 4 3 4\n1 4 1 4 2\n1 4 4 4 3\nOutput\n166374062\n\n\n-----Note-----\n\nLet\'s look at all possible orders of cities Monuments will be build in:\n\n$[1, 2, 3]$:\n\nthe first city controls all points at distance at most $3$, in other words, points $1$ and $4$;\n\nthe second city controls all points at distance at most $2$, or points $1$, $3$ and $5$;\n\nthe third city controls all points at distance at most $1$, or point $1$.\n\nIn total, $4$ points are controlled.\n\n$[1, 3, 2]$: the first city controls points $1$ and $4$; the second city — points $1$ and $3$; the third city — point $1$. In total, $3$ points.\n\n$[2, 1, 3]$: the first city controls point $1$; the second city — points $1$, $3$ and $5$; the third city — point $1$. In total, $3$ points.\n\n$[2, 3, 1]$: the first city controls point $1$; the second city — points $1$, $3$ and $5$; the third city — point $1$. In total, $3$ points.\n\n$[3, 1, 2]$: the first city controls point $1$; the second city — points $1$ and $3$; the third city — points $1$ and $5$. In total, $3$ points.\n\n$[3, 2, 1]$: the first city controls point $1$; the second city — points $1$, $3$ and $5$; the third city — points $1$ and $5$. In total, $3$ points.\n\nThe expected number of controlled points is $\\frac{4 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3}{6}$ $=$ $\\frac{19}{6}$ or $19 \\cdot 6^{-1}$ $\\equiv$ $19 \\cdot 166374059$ $\\equiv$ $166374062$ $\\pmod{998244353}$\n\nRead the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs).  #### Responses to be Scored #### [The Begin of Response 1]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine the expected number of points that Monocarp will conquer after building monuments in all cities, considering the random selection of cities for each turn. The solution involves calculating the probability that each point is covered by at least one monument, taking into account the random permutation of city selections and the increasing coverage radius of each monument over time.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Understanding Monument Coverage**: Each monument in a city expands its coverage radius by 1 each turn. A monument in city `i` can cover a point `j` if it is built on turn `t` where `t >= distance(i, j)`.\n2. **Probability Calculation**: For each point, we need to calculate the probability that it is covered by at least one monument. This involves checking if there exists at least one city that can cover the point when built on a sufficiently late turn.\n3. **Dynamic Programming (DP) with Bitmasking**: To efficiently count valid permutations where cities are placed in positions that meet the required coverage criteria, we use dynamic programming with bitmasking to track used positions.\n4. **Modular Arithmetic**: Given the constraints, we use modular arithmetic to handle large numbers and ensure results fit within the required modulus.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\n\nMOD = 998244353\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    n = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    m = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n\n    d = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        row = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr + m]))\n        ptr += m\n        d.append(row)\n\n    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials\n    fact = [1] * (n + 1)\n    for i in range(1, n + 1):\n        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD\n\n    inv_fact = [1] * (n + 1)\n    inv_fact[n] = pow(fact[n], MOD - 2, MOD)\n    for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):\n        inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1) % MOD\n\n    total = 0\n    for j in range(m):\n        # Compute S_j and L_all\n        S_j = []\n        for i in range(n):\n            if d[i][j] <= n:\n                S_j.append(i)\n        k = len(S_j)\n        if k == 0:\n            # This point is never covered\n            total = (total + 1) % MOD\n            continue\n\n        # Precompute is_in_S and L_all\n        is_in_S = [False] * n\n        for city in S_j:\n            is_in_S[city] = True\n        L_all = [0] * n\n        for idx, city in enumerate(S_j):\n            L_all[city] = d[city][j] - 1\n\n        # DP\n        dp = [0] * (1 << n)\n        dp[0] = 1\n        for mask in range(1 << n):\n            if dp[mask] == 0:\n                continue\n            for bit in range(n):\n                if not (mask & (1 << bit)):\n                    new_mask = mask | (1 << bit)\n                    pos = bin(mask).count(\'1\') + 1\n                    if is_in_S[bit]:\n                        if pos > L_all[bit]:\n                            continue\n                    dp[new_mask] = (dp[new_mask] + dp[mask]) % MOD\n\n        X = dp[(1 << n) - 1]\n        numerator = (fact[n] - X) % MOD\n        contribution = numerator * inv_fact[n] % MOD\n        total = (total + contribution) % MOD\n\n    print(total)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading Input**: The input is read and parsed into a matrix of distances from cities to points.\n2. **Factorial Precomputation**: Factorials and their modular inverses are precomputed up to `n` to facilitate quick calculations of combinations and probabilities.\n3. **Dynamic Programming with Bitmasking**: For each point, we use dynamic programming with bitmasking to count valid permutations where cities are placed in positions that meet the required coverage criteria. This involves checking if placing a city in a specific position is allowed based on its distance to the point.\n4. **Modular Arithmetic**: The solution uses modular arithmetic to handle large numbers and ensure results fit within the required modulus, providing the final result as an irreducible fraction modulo 998244353.\n

 [The Begin of Response 2]
\nTo solve this problem, we need to determine the expected number of points that Monocarp will conquer after building monuments in all cities. The solution involves calculating the probability that each point is covered by at least one monument, considering the random permutation of city selections and the increasing coverage radius of each monument over time.\n\n### Approach\n1. **Understanding Monument Coverage**: Each monument in a city expands its coverage radius by 1 each turn. A monument in city `i` can cover a point `j` if it is built on turn `t` where `t >= distance(i, j)`.\n2. **Probability Calculation**: For each point, we calculate the probability that it is covered by at least one monument. This involves checking if there exists at least one city that can cover the point when built on a sufficiently late turn.\n3. **Dynamic Programming (DP) with Bitmasking**: To efficiently count valid permutations where cities are placed in positions that meet the required coverage criteria, we use dynamic programming with bitmasking to track used positions.\n4. **Modular Arithmetic**: Given the constraints, we use modular arithmetic to handle large numbers and ensure results fit within the required modulus.\n\n### Solution Code\n```python\nimport sys\n\nMOD = 998244353\n\ndef main():\n    input = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    ptr = 0\n    n = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n    m = int(input[ptr])\n    ptr += 1\n\n    d = []\n    for _ in range(n):\n        row = list(map(int, input[ptr:ptr + m]))\n        ptr += m\n        d.append(row)\n\n    # Precompute factorial and inverse factorial up to n\n    max_fact = n\n    fact = [1] * (max_fact + 1)\n    for i in range(1, max_fact + 1):\n        fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD\n\n    inv_fact = [1] * (max_fact + 1)\n    inv_fact[max_fact] = pow(fact[max_fact], MOD-2, MOD)\n    for i in range(max_fact-1, -1, -1):\n        inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) % MOD\n\n    total = 0\n\n    for j in range(m):\n        # Collect cities i where d[i][j] <= n\n        A = []\n        for i in range(n):\n            if d[i][j] <= n:\n                A.append(i)\n        k = len(A)\n        if k == 0:\n            continue\n        # Check if any a_i is zero\n        a_list = [d[i][j] - 1 for i in A]\n        if any(x == 0 for x in a_list):\n            total = (total + 1) % MOD\n            continue\n        # Sort by a_i in increasing order\n        A_sorted = sorted(zip(a_list, A), key=lambda x: x[0])\n        a_list_sorted = [x[0] for x in A_sorted]\n        cities = [x[1] for x in A_sorted]\n        # Perform DP\n        dp = [0] * (1 << n)\n        dp[0] = 1\n        for i in range(k):\n            current_a = a_list_sorted[i]\n            new_dp = [0] * (1 << n)\n            for mask in range(1 << n):\n                if dp[mask] == 0:\n                    continue\n                for p in range(1, current_a + 1):\n                    if not (mask & (1 << (p - 1))):\n                        new_mask = mask | (1 << (p - 1))\n                        new_dp[new_mask] = (new_dp[new_mask] + dp[mask]) % MOD\n            dp = new_dp\n        total_ways = sum(dp) % MOD\n        if k == 0:\n            pass\n        else:\n            fact_n_minus_k = fact[n - k]\n            inv_denominator = inv_fact[n]\n            numerator = total_ways * fact_n_minus_k % MOD\n            prob_not_covered = numerator * inv_denominator % MOD\n            contribution = (1 - prob_not_covered) % MOD\n            total = (total + contribution) % MOD\n    print(total)\n\nif __name__ == "__main__":\n    main()\n```\n\n### Explanation\n1. **Reading Input**: The input is read and parsed into a matrix of distances from cities to points.\n2. **Factorial Precomputation**: Factorials and their modular inverses are precomputed up to `n` to facilitate quick calculations of combinations and probabilities.\n3. **Dynamic Programming with Bitmasking**: For each point, we use dynamic programming with bitmasking to count valid permutations of city placements that meet the coverage criteria.\n4. **Probability Calculation**: For each point, the probability that it is not covered is calculated using the dynamic programming results. The expected number of points conquered is derived using modular arithmetic to handle large numbers efficiently.\n\nThis approach ensures that we efficiently compute the expected number of points conquered, even with the constraints of the problem.\n
